# Front Cover

->**[Exterior Binding]**<-

<image-desc>The volume is bound in dark brown, textured leather, featuring elaborate <term>blind-stamped</term><note>A decorative technique where patterns are impressed into leather without the use of gold leaf or ink.</note> decorative elements. The design consists of a rectangular frame formed by multiple parallel lines running near the edges. Within the corners of this frame are stylized floral motifs. At the center of the cover is a prominent, vertically-oriented decorative <term>cartouche</term><note>An ornamental frame or scroll-like design, often used to highlight a central feature.</note> with a complex, symmetrical outline. Inside this central cartouche is a stylized <term>foliate</term><note>Leaf-like or plant-based ornamentation.</note> design. The leather itself has a pronounced pebbled grain.</image-desc>

<summary>The front cover of the manuscript, featuring a sophisticated dark brown leather binding with blind-stamped floral and geometric decorations typical of historical bookbinding.</summary>

<keywords>bookbinding, embossed leather, blind-stamped, cartouche, foliate ornament, manuscript exterior</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page serves as the symbolic frontispiece for "The Golden Fleece" (La Toyson d'Or), a famous alchemical treatise. It establishes the visual and philosophical foundation for the work, focusing on the transformation of matter and the search for the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

->THE GOLDEN FLEECE<-

---

<term>Visit the interior of the earth; by rectification, you will find the hidden stone <note>original Latin: "Visita Interiora Terrae Rectificando Invenies Occultum Lapidem." This is a famous alchemical acronym for VITRIOL, suggesting that the secret of transformation lies deep within the "earth" of the self or raw matter.</note></term>

---

If you go from the small
to all the greatness that the small [contains]
this well-prepared table
can be found at the end. <note>This verse suggests that by starting with humble materials and following the correct "table" or process, the alchemist reaches the ultimate goal.</note>

<summary>The title page of an alchemical manuscript featuring the famous VITRIOL acronym and allegorical imagery related to the "Golden Fleece" of the philosophers. It emphasizes the journey from the microscopic or humble beginning to the great work of the hidden stone.</summary>

<keywords>Golden Fleece, VITRIOL, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, rectification, planetary symbols, transmutation</keywords>

THE
# GOLDEN FLEECE
->OR<-
## THE FLOWER OF TREA-
## SURES,
IN WHICH IS SUCCINC-
tly and methodically treated
the Philosopher's Stone, its ex-
cellence, effects, and admirable virtue.

->PLUS<-

->Of its Origin, and of the true means of being able to arrive at perfection.<-

## ENRICHED WITH FIGURES, AND THE
proper Colors represented to the life, according as they must
necessarily occur in the practice of this beautiful Work <note>The "Great Work" or "Magnum Opus" refers to the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

->AND<-

->*Gathered from the most grave monuments of Antiquity, as much Chaldean, Hebrew, Egyptian, Arabian, Greek, as Latin, and other approved Authors.*<-

# By that Great Philosopher SOLOMON
# TRISMOSIN Preceptor of Paracelsus <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a famous Swiss physician and alchemist; Trismosin is his legendary teacher.</note>.

->*Translated from German into French, and commented upon in the form of a Paraphrase on each Chapter by L. I.*<-

## AT PARIS,
## At the house of CHARLES SEVESTRE, St. James Street before the Mathurins.

---

->1612<-
->*WITH THE PRIVILEGE OF THE KING*<-

<summary>This is the title page of the 1612 French edition of "The Golden Fleece," a foundational alchemical treatise attributed to Solomon Trismosin, the legendary teacher of Paracelsus. It promises a methodical explanation of the Philosopher's Stone, complete with color illustrations of the chemical stages.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>The previous page was a blank flyleaf. This page begins the formal dedication of the work to François de Bourbon, Prince of Conti.</meta>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut banner featuring symmetrical scrolling floral patterns and foliate motifs.</image-desc>

# TO

## THE MOST HIGH AND MOST
## ILLUSTRIOUS PRINCE, MY
## LORD FRANÇOIS DE
## BOURBON, PRINCE OF CONTI,

### Sovereign of Château-Renaud, and
### Lands beyond and on this side of the Meuse,
### Governor and Lieutenant General
### of the King in the Lands of Anjou, Tou-
### raine, and Maine, etc.

---

My Lord,
*Those who, driven by some lofty*
*ambition, rashly offer*
*the vows of their steadfastness*
*under the somber appearance of many whims,*
*promise themselves nothing less—through the contrived*
*excess of a spirit bold in speech <note>original: "fort en bouche," implying one who talks a great deal but lacks action</note>—than a firm assurance*
*of complete and fortunate success; yet when*
*they fail to achieve this vainly*
*imagined prosperity, they are often*
*forced to change their tune and judge differently,*
*owing to a disadvantage quickly cast upon the*
*fickleness of immoderate passions—an outcome*
*which the sad result of that first impression had not*
*anticipated; thus they regret at leisure the course of their ill-conceived errors.*

<sig>A ij</sig>

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of the book to François de Bourbon, Prince of Conti, listing his noble titles and territories. The author begins a philosophical opening regarding the dangers of boastful ambition and the fickleness of human passion.</summary>
<keywords>François de Bourbon, Prince of Conti, dedication, patronage, Château-Renaud, Anjou, Touraine, Maine, 17th-century rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the long, flowing opening sentence of the dedication to the Prince of Conti from page 3, expanding on the metaphorical dangers of pride and ambition.</meta>

*against the grain of the hope they had imag-*
*ined for themselves in a delightful slumber: when in the*
*middle of their course, sailing without thinking into*
*the highest sea of their own conceptions, one*
*sees them carried away at the whim of the winds, as if by the*
*waxed wings of some ambitious Icarus, charmed*
*by the dark shadows of ignorance <note>original: "mescognoissance," implying a lack of self-knowledge or a failure to recognize the reality of one's situation</note>,*
*and running the risk of both their fortune and*
*their life, upon the impetuous back of a Neptune*
*angered by the riot of waves hostile to their*
*happiness, which the haughty <note>original: "sourcilleuses," literally "frowning" or "brow-knitting," used here to describe the high, intimidating crests of waves</note> heads of the*
*wandering waves have proudly raised up to the*
*Heaven of their misery, only to plunge them into the*
*deep gulfs of the senseless waves, taking pleasure*
*with impunity in the misfortune of their vessel.*
*There is not one among them who, feeling himself two fingers*
*from the toll <note>original: "peage," used here as a metaphor for the final reckoning, death, or the "price" one pays at the end of a journey</note>, does not lose his judgment, and at the*
*same time abandon the reins of his prudence,*
*to yield to the violent fits of such an emotion,*
*so greatly altered by the storms of despair,*
*that their first tracks <note>original: "brisees," a hunting term referring to broken branches used to mark a trail; here it means their initial plans or path</note> lose their hold on*
*this arena <note>original: "lice," referring to the enclosed field for a tournament or combat</note>, undertaken to oppose the*
*harsh symptoms of the storm, under the confident*
*protection of an expert mariner <note>original: "nautonnier"</note>, whose skill*
*contended with open force for the remainder of*
*their life, resigned into his hands, while they*
*made a truce with the care of their journey, in order*

<summary>The author uses vivid maritime and mythological metaphors—specifically Icarus and Neptune—to describe the downfall of ambitious men who lose themselves in "delightful slumbers" of pride. He depicts the moment a "vessel" of ambition is overtaken by a storm, where even the most expert mariner struggles to save those who have abandoned their own prudence.</summary>
<keywords>Icarus, Neptune, nautical metaphor, ambition, fate, maritime imagery, 17th-century rhetoric, Fortune</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page concludes the extended nautical metaphor from the previous page, where the author compares the dangers of pride and intellectual ambition to a storm at sea. It then transitions into the author's explanation of the allegory as a reflection of his own difficult research process.</meta>

to receive from this pitiless monster <note>Referring to the "Neptune" or personified storm mentioned on the previous page</note> such a condition of living or dying as the severity of its misfortunes would sadly dare to impose upon them.

*Thus confused, and already almost reduced to the*
*final sighs of a forced necessity, the joyful*
*Halcyons <note>Kingfishers; in classical mythology, these birds were said to calm the sea during the winter solstice to nest, thus becoming symbols of peace and hope</note>, advance-messengers of the Favonian <note>Favonius was the Roman name for the West Wind, typically regarded as a gentle, favorable spring breeze</note> air,*
*appearing amidst the harsh aspect of these*
*strong shocks, made them cast their eyes toward*
*a ship happily dedicated to the pursuit of their*
*salvation, which—calling back quite opportunely*
*from the terrifying sepulcher of the waters*
*these weakened bodies, rescued at the price of*
*a few broken planks—placed them by an unhoped-*
*for favor on the shore of their aspirations. The*
*shipwreck avoided made them return once more,*
*in the same state they were in at the first stage*
*of their journey; and the close benevolence of*
*stars that were now more tranquil, raising their*
*spirits already beaten down under the powerful*
*efforts of apprehension, opens for them the path*
*of green laurels <note>The traditional symbol of victory, honor, and poetic achievement</note>, which they finally found scattered*
*within the living nursery of their perseverance.*

This vivid and long-winded portrait <note>original: "Tableau de longue haleine," suggesting a complex, sustained artistic or literary effort</note>, represented upon the very theater of the imagination, prudently hides beneath the veil of its allegorical figure a stray model of my own anxieties, in order to bring to light this labor of mine, the product of painstaking research. Those who have been happily saved...

<sig>A iij</sig> <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of a book in the correct order.</note>

<vocab>Halcyons, Favonian, shipwreck, allegorical, labor, perseverance</vocab>

<summary>The author concludes a metaphor about surviving a storm at sea, representing the transition from despair to success through perseverance. He then reveals that this allegory is a reflection of his own difficult intellectual struggle in completing his research.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, perseverance, Halcyons, Favonian winds, scholarly labor, navigation metaphor, maritime imagery</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's transition from the nautical allegory to a personal reflection on his intellectual and spiritual journey. He addresses potential critics and explains his process of gathering philosophical wisdom as a "honey" collected for the reader.</meta>

born of the plains of slander <note>original: "plaines meſdiſantes," likely referring to those who dwell in the realm of gossip and critical judgment</note> in similar circumstances, may well be stirred by the stings of my anxiety, after having always fearfully sounded the depths of sharp-pointed waves; but ignorance and timidity will remain indifferent to the stirrings of my compassion. Only the Athlete <note>In this context, an "athlete of the mind" or a seasoned seeker who has undergone great trials</note>—whose courage and confidence are often made a prey to misfortune—can settle our dispute through the skill of his experience. Had I not tasted these lures, I could not judge their bitterness; and the wormwood <note>original: "abſynthe," a bitter herb representing sorrow or harsh reality</note> of jealous rigors would not have attacked the sweet myrrh <note>A fragrant resin used for healing and preservation; here representing the author's protective virtues</note> of my remedies, if the ocean of my constancy had not bent the back of my labors upon the shifting sands of their audacity.

It is true that satisfaction and leisure led me with a single leap toward this recreation: to prepare the vessel of a high science <term>high science|A term often used for alchemy, metaphysics, or the "Great Work" of intellectual and spiritual perfection</term> to navigate all the coasts of this vast Universe, and to gather from every flower of the best Philosophers a swarm of sweet honey to present to you. Here, the pilots <note>original: "nochers," meaning navigators or mariners</note> of my designs—born of curiosity and holding absolute command over the preparations for the Fleece <note>A reference to the Golden Fleece of the Argonauts, a common alchemical symbol for the ultimate prize of wisdom or the Philosopher's Stone</note>—have used my solar pen <note>A metaphor for a divinely inspired or brilliant style of writing, possibly linked to the sun as a symbol of clarity and truth</note> to row more swiftly across the true horoscope <note>original: "horoſcope véritable," referring here to the "guiding map" or destined path found within the works of authoritative writers</note> of good authors. Indeed, my spirit was equipped, so it seemed to me, with all necessary things (or rather

<summary>The author explains that only those who have experienced intellectual or spiritual struggle can truly judge his work. He describes his project as a voyage to gather the "honey" of wisdom from various philosophers, guided by a "solar pen" toward the "Golden Fleece" of high science.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, Golden Fleece, Alchemical allegory, Intellectual struggle, Wisdom, Experience vs Ignorance, Solar pen</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s nautical metaphor. On the previous page, he described the "pilots of his designs" preparing a vessel of "high science" to gather wisdom like honey. Here, the vessel—and the author’s own frankness—is caught in a storm of slander and criticism, ultimately being rescued by the "remembrance" and favor of his patron.</meta>

dazzled by my own satisfactions) was exposing itself 
to the fair wind it had already gathered from its labor, 
upon the slanderous sea of this world, without otherwise 
foreseeing the force of the storm, which followed 
very closely upon the uncertain steps of my frankness <note>original: "franchise," referring here to his open and honest intellectual expression</note>, 
through the indiscreet liberty of venomous strikes and 
bites. Yet I did not wish, enveloped in these 
sudden encounters, to leave fallow <note>original: "en friche," a farming term meaning uncultivated; the author refuses to let his work go to waste despite the attacks</note> the 
modest commerce of my aspirations, opposing 
the nets of their rigor with the consecutive snares of 
my perseverance: but finally succumbing 
under the troublesome burden of so many storms, I saw 
the hour where I fell between the slanderous shackles 
of their presumption, and the broken sails of my 
frigate <note>A small, fast ship; here symbolizing the author's work or intellectual journey</note>, abandoned to the whim of my censors <note>original: "censeurs," meaning critics or those who judge the works of others</note>, 
were preparing the triumph of my captivity for my 
misfortune. It was upon this last plank <note>original: "deniere table," likely referring to the *tabula naufragii*, the legal and metaphorical "last plank" of safety for a shipwrecked sailor</note> that 
my near-shipwreck had need of your favors; 
it was in this combat—not one-on-one, nor out 
of sight like the <term>Andabates|A class of Roman gladiators who fought blindly, their helmets having no eye-slits</term>, but of one man 
against all where I saw myself surprised, not 
having known how to encounter so suddenly, for 
the rescue of my troubles, the man such as the 
<term>Cynic Sophist|A reference to the philosopher Diogenes of Sinope, who famously carried a lamp in broad daylight, searching for an "honest man"</term> sought 
so carefully at high noon with the curious torch 
of his desires: but the oar of my good 
fortune, having led me, in this unequal match, 
to the Fortunate Isles <note>Mythical islands of the blessed; here representing the safe harbor of the patron’s intellectual or social circle</note> of your remembrance, bless—

<summary>The author describes falling victim to harsh critics and "slanderous storms" that nearly destroyed his work, comparing his rescue to finding a safe harbor in the favor and memory of his patron.</summary>

benignly withdrew me from the precipice of the ill-disposed,

brilliant rays of your generous power,

mere memory of your grave Virtues, ren-
dering the air once again serene for me, and the trident of the sea

for the first time from these dangerous vipers,
the fief of my protection is held in homage to
your piety; what less could I do in this
second distress <note>original: "destroit," referring to a narrow strait or a state of difficulty</note>, than to run to the same
vows which were already once before health-
ful to me? To these ends, My Lord <note>original: "Monseigneur"</note>, I prostrate
the new fruits of my tree of Hermes <note>A reference to Hermeticism or alchemy; Hermes Trismegistus was the legendary founder of these "secret" sciences</note>,
at the respectful feet of your illustrious Name,
to breathe benignly upon the simplicity of these
fearful lines the necessary breath of your
authority and the agreeable liquor of your sweetness,
so that the venom of the injurious harpies <note>In mythology, "the snatchers"—winged monsters who stole food; here, the author uses them to represent predatory critics</note>, sliding

and sweet flavor. But as the subject is
of importance and elevated, so too does it have need for

[tunate in the] tutelary protection of your worthy favors, I will no longer have

# THE KING’S PRIVILEGE.

<image-desc>Decorative drop-cap letter 'L' featuring an elaborate floral and foliate pattern set within a square frame.</image-desc>

Louis <note>Louis XIII, who was a child at this time; the kingdom was under the regency of his mother, Marie de' Medici</note> BY THE GRACE OF GOD, KING of France and of Navarre, To our beloved and loyal Counselors, the members of our Court of the Parlement of Paris, and to all our other Justices and Officers, Greetings. Our dear and well-beloved Charles Sevestre, merchant Bookseller <term>Libraire: in this period, a person who functioned as both a publisher and a seller of books</term> residing in our city of Paris, has humbly petitioned us, showing that there has been placed into his hands a book titled, *The Golden Fleece, or the Flower of Treasures <note>original: "La Toyson d’or, ou la fleur des Thresors"</note> enriched with figures, and gathered from the most grave monuments of antiquity, by that great Philosopher Salomon Trismosin <note>A legendary alchemical figure, famously claimed to be the teacher of Paracelsus</note>, Preceptor of Paracelsus, translated from German into French by L. I.* Which he desires to have printed and brought to light: but he fears that others besides himself, or those to whom the said petitioner has given charge to do so, might presume to have it printed, thus depriving him of his expenses and labors, unless he were provided with our suitable letters to this effect. FOR THIS REASON, desiring to assist our subjects according to the requirements of the case, and wishing...

<summary>This page contains the opening of the "Privilege du Roy," a legal monopoly granted by King Louis XIII to the publisher Charles Sevestre for the printing of an alchemical treatise attributed to Salomon Trismosin. It establishes the legal protection against unauthorized copying of the work.</summary>
<keywords>Royal Privilege, Charles Sevestre, Salomon Trismosin, Paracelsus, alchemy, publishing law, Paris, 17th century</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page concludes the official "Privilège du Roi" (Royal Privilege) that began on the previous page. It details the legal protections and penalties granted to the publisher Charles Sevestre to protect his investment in printing Salomon Trismosin's alchemical work.</meta>

the said petitioner to be compensated for his expenses, outlays, pains, and labors, we have permitted and granted, and do permit and grant by these presents, the right to print or have printed, sell, and distribute throughout our Kingdom the said book. No person other than the said petitioner, or those holding rights or power from him, may print or have it printed, sold, or distributed until the term of six years has passed, starting from the day and date of printing. This is under penalty of confiscation <note>the seizure of unauthorized copies</note>, an arbitrary fine <note>a fine set at the discretion of a judge rather than a fixed statutory amount</note>, and all costs, damages, and interests owed to him.

We further will that by placing, or causing to be placed by him, at the beginning or at the end of the said book these presents or a brief extract thereof, they shall be held as officially notified and brought to the knowledge of all, without suffering or allowing any hindrance to be made, placed, or given to him to the contrary. **FOR SUCH IS OUR PLEASURE** <note>original: "CAR AINSY NOVS PLAIST IL" — the standard formulaic ending for royal edicts in France, asserting the King's absolute authority</note> that it be done, notwithstanding any letters or orders to the contrary. Given at Paris on the eighth day of October, in the year of grace one thousand six hundred and twelve, and of our Reign the third.

->BY THE KING.<-
->POVSSEPIN.<-

propriately, Propriety—in order to avoid venomous blame and the censure of a distrustful public—places before our eyes this point of modesty:

# Prologue.

So scatterbrained are they, that being possessed by a great portion of the Moon's whims <note>original: "caprices de la Lune". To be under the influence of the moon's whims was a common way to describe someone who is "lunatic" or prone to foolish, shifting moods.</note>, they break their heads thinking they can make her descend, along with her influences, onto the body of the Earth—the mother of the Elements—even by a path they never knew. They are supported only by the natural appearances of a covetous curiosity that is hungry for novelties. But if it is true that *there is no desire for what is unknown* <note>original: "ignoti nulla cupido". A Latin maxim often attributed to the poet Ovid, though the author here credits "the Philosopher," likely referring to Aristotle.</note>, as the Philosopher says, what image can they possibly conceive of the transcendent effects of our good Genius <note>original: "Genie". In this context, it refers to a guiding spirit or the innate intellectual power required for the Great Work of alchemy.</note>?

> *Their Spirit, lighter than a drifting cloud,*
> *Cannot speak well of things wrapped in a shroud.*

And just as the blind cannot judge colors because they are deprived of sight, so too can the ignorant only speak of the <term>Sky of the Philosophers<note>original: "Ciel des Philosophes". An alchemical term for the pure, quintessence-filled state of matter, or the highest level of understanding in the Art.</note></term> while stammering, or while sitting idly with their feet under the table: *If the fates call you—* <note>original: "Si te fata vo-". A partial quote from Virgil's Aeneid (6.147): *Si te fata vocant*. It refers to the Golden Bough, which can only be plucked by those destined by fate. The text cuts off here to be continued on the next page.</note>

<summary>The author mocks those who attempt alchemy without true knowledge, comparing their baseless curiosity to the delusions of the "moon-struck" and the sightless judging colors.</summary>

...call you, otherwise not <note>original: "aliter non". Part of a Latin quote from Virgil's Aeneid suggesting that success only comes to those destined for it.</note>, says Augurel in his *Gold-making* <note>original: "Chrysopee", referring to Giovanni Aurelio Augurello’s 1515 poem *Chrysopoeia*, which used Latin verse to describe the creation of gold.</note>.

This bitterness will depart and return no more.

I shall not stop the world from slandering,
Rather I wish to plead my case before them:
I take them as witnesses that I intend to say nothing
That is not in good taste, nor do I wish to provoke them.

Whoever can do better must publish it,
And give this Treasure <note>original: "Thresor"; a common synonym for the Philosophers' Stone or the secret knowledge of its creation.</note> to posterity:
But discretion does not suggest he should align himself
With a slanderous vice full of arrogance.

To criticize is easy, to do better is difficult,
And a critic <note>original: "Censeur"</note> is always driven by some passion:
Yet all things considered, let them bite one by one <note>original: "file à file"; meaning one after another or in succession.</note>,
For I shall remain firm in my good intentions.

-># The Golden Fleece.<-

The **Stone of the Wise** <note>original: "Pierre des Sages", another name for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is like the human body, which transforms lower forms and those of different conditions into the purity of its own substance. This is achieved by means of this natural and tempered fire, which is the true governor and the only guide for our **great vessel** <note>In alchemy, the "great vessel" refers to the specialized flask where the transformation occurs, but also serves as a metaphor for the human body or the universe itself.</note>; **the lesser fire wears all things away** <note>original Latin: "minor ignis omnia terit". This alchemical maxim suggests that a gentle, steady heat is more effective at breaking down and refining matter than a violent flame.</note>. It is the pilot and the **radical moisture** <term>Radical Moisture: The fundamental, life-sustaining fluid believed to exist within all matter, acting as the "glue" that allows different elements to unite and grow without becoming brittle.</term> where diverse natures live peacefully, and where many contrary qualities and differing discords compose chords of harmony. These are brought together through the labor of a necessary **coction** <term>Coction: From the Latin 'coquere' (to cook); the process of maturing or "digesting" a substance through the steady application of heat.</term> and a moist heat, which act in equal proportion upon these metallic bodies.

*The body transforms all into its own nature,*
*Whatever is given to it serves as its nourishment:*
*Our Work does the same with imperfect metals,*
*Making them equal to its most perfect Kings.* <note>In alchemical symbolism, "Kings" typically refer to gold or the most refined and perfected state of matter.</note>

## Concerning the first doctrine of this **Art**.

# SECOND FIGURE.

This illustration depicts a venerable philosopher, often identified in this tradition as Solomon Trismosin himself, portrayed with a long white beard to signify the deep wisdom and time required to master the <term>Great Work</term> <note>The "Great Work" (Magnus Opus) is the alchemical term for the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. He is dressed in a red tunic and a heavy brown mantle, standing upon a small patch of earth where two medicinal or symbolic plants grow, representing the "living" nature of his materials. 

In his left hand, he holds a dark green, pear-shaped glass vessel—the essential tool of the **Art**—while pointing to it with his right hand to emphasize that the entire mystery is contained within this single container. Behind him, a large, blank, winding scroll unfurls across the dark background. This empty scroll suggests the "silent" or unwritten knowledge of the **Philosophers**, which cannot be fully communicated through words alone but must be realized through practice and divine inspiration.

# THIRD TREATISE
### of the said <term>Work</term>. <note>The "Work" (Grand Œuvre) refers to the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy.</note>

The great Genius of our Science and father of the highest and rarest philosophy, <term>Hermes</term>, <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure considered the founder of the "hermetic" arts (alchemy and astrology).</note> rising within himself and engaging his spirit on the operation of the work of the Philosophers, finally uttered these words: [This may be described as an end of the world, in that heaven and earth produce well together, but no one can through heaven and earth know our two preceding doctrines, veiled by so many <term>Hieroglyphs</term>.] <note>In alchemical literature, "hieroglyphs" are not just Egyptian script, but any symbolic images used to hide chemical secrets from the uninitiated.</note> Many also who have reached this labor have toiled and sweated greatly before capturing this perfection; having attained it, they explain it afterward, but with many <term>amphibological</term> <note>Derived from "amphibology," meaning a phrase that can be interpreted in two ways; alchemists intentionally used such double-meanings to obscure their instructions.</note> ambiguities, and so confused that they cannot be understood, by

### Third Similitude.

Avicenna <note>Avicenna (c. 980–1037) was a renowned Persian polymath; in the Renaissance, many alchemical treatises were attributed to him, focusing on the chemical properties of minerals and the "soul" of metals.</note>, treating moisture and all its effects, says that one perceives in the first place some blackness <note>In alchemy, this "noirceur" refers to the *nigredo* or "blackening" stage, which represents the death and decomposition of the base material before it can be purified.</note>, when heat performs its operation on moist bodies. This is why the Ancient Sages, without otherwise unraveling the ambiguity of their enigmatic figures <note>Alchemists famously used "enigmatic figures"—riddles and complex metaphors—to hide their secrets from the uninitiated.</note>, say they have observed from afar a rising fog, surrounding the whole earth and making it moist; they also say they have foreseen the great impetuosity of the sea and the abundant gathering of waters swimming over the entire face of the earth, such that form and matter—deprived of their original strength and filled with putrefaction <note>The process of "putrefaction" was seen as necessary for the "rebirth" of the substance; it is the breakdown of the physical form to release its inner essence.</note>—will see themselves shaken amidst the very darkness, even unto the King of the Earth <note>The "King of the Earth" is a symbolic figure often representing the solid, metallic principle or the "prime matter" undergoing the agony of transformation.</note>, whom they will hear crying out and lamenting in a piteous voice, full of

# The Golden Fleece.

### Fourth Similitude.

Menaldus <note>Menaldus (often identified with the philosopher Merualdus) is a recurring figure in the *Turba Philosophorum*, an ancient alchemical text that gathers the opinions of various sages.</note> clearly demonstrates the necessity and close connection that living things have with dead things in these words:

> "I wish," he says, "and intend that all those who devote themselves to our serious study, and who desire to follow absolutely the same order and path that we have held and duly observed to our satisfaction, should act in such a way that spiritual things <note>In alchemy, "spiritual" things are "volatile" substances—those that easily evaporate or turn into gas when heated.</note> become corporeal <note>To "corporatize" or make corporeal means to "fix" a substance, turning a gas or liquid into a stable solid that can endure fire.</note>, and that corporeal things also become spiritualized by a reciprocal conversion and dissipation of their primary forms, in order to acquire a more excellent one, rising from this death—which is putrefaction <note>Putrefaction is the "blackening" stage of the alchemical process (*nigredo*), where the matter must "die" and decay before it can be reborn in a purified state.</note>—much more glorious than before."

<summary>This page presents the "Fourth Similitude" by the philosopher Menaldus, outlining the core alchemical requirement of "fixing the volatile and volatilizing the fixed." He describes a cycle of death and rebirth where matter must undergo putrefaction to achieve a more glorious, perfected state.</summary>
<keywords>Menaldus, spiritualization, corporalization, putrefaction, transformation, alchemy, Turba Philosophorum</keywords>

# The Golden Fleece.

For if it <note>The "it" refers back to the "spirit" or "soul" discussed on the previous page, which acts as the transformative agent during the alchemical process.</note> remains firm within the body, and renders anew those bodies—which are by nature earthly, massive, and coarse—into something spiritual through the power of these spirits <term>spirits: in alchemy, these are volatile substances or vapors that can penetrate and transform solid matter.</term>, this is the very goal of our Work <term>Work: often called the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus), it is the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</term>.

### Sixth Similitude.

The discourses of the most **discreet** <note>original: *discrets*. In alchemical literature, "discreet" refers to the "Sages" or "Adepts" who were bound by oath to keep the secrets of the Art hidden from the uninitiated.</note> are always **ambiguous**, and their grave writings [are] always **intermingled** <note>original: *entre-meſlez*. This describes the "scattering" technique used by alchemists, where they would intentionally shuffle the steps of a process or mix metaphors to ensure only a dedicated student could decipher the true meaning.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Sixth Similitude, cautioning the reader that alchemical masters intentionally use ambiguous and interwoven language to protect their secrets, as symbolized by the dual-natured winged figure.</summary>

Because the essence of our **Work** <note>The "Great Work" (*Magnum Opus*) of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> draws its strength from **contrary qualities** perfectly united. **Rhazes** <note>original: "Rafis." Yes, since the earth contains fire within itself, just as air is contained within water. **Rhazes** <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and one of the most influential figures in the history of medicine and alchemy.</note> warns us likewise that certain "softenings" of the Art <note>Refers to the alchemical craft; "softening" here means the process of making a solid substance pliable or liquid.</note> must precede the perfect operation of the **Work** <note>The *Magnum Opus* or "Great Work," the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. ### *First Article.*

<image-desc>Intricate decorative woodcut initial L featuring floral scrollwork and foliate motifs.</image-desc>The first step established by the <term>Alchemists</term> <note>Practitioners of the "Art of Transmutation," seeking to purify materials and the soul.</note> to reach the golden Summit of our beautiful work, is called by those most expert in this <term>Hermetic</term> <note>Refers to the tradition of Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy.</note> art, <term>Solution</term> <note>The process of dissolving a solid substance into a liquid state.</note>, which requires, according to Nature herself, that the Body be boiled until perfect <term>coction</term> <note>From the Latin *coctio*, meaning cooking or ripening through heat.</note>. All our <term>magistery</term> <note>original: *magistère*; the mastery or technical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is nothing but cooking: *Cook, cook, and cook again, and let it not weary you* <note>original Latin: *Coque, coque, & iterum coque, nec te tædeat*; a famous alchemical maxim emphasizing patience and the transformative power of heat.</note>. Pontanus <note>Johannes Pontanus (often identified as a 15th-century physician/alchemist) is famous in esoteric literature for his "Epistle," where he confesses to failing for three years because he used the wrong fire.</note> knows well what to say to us about it, when in one of his Epistles he wishes to make us wise through his own perils (if the faults of others can cause us to pause); he who by this single defect was distanced out of sight from his goals. the dittany of healing. <note>original: *le dyctame de guariſon*. Dittany was a legendary medicinal herb, famously used by Venus in Virgil's *Aeneid* to heal Aeneas. In alchemy, it signifies the restorative power found within the matter itself.</note> Fire that is too violent ruins what it encounters; moderate fire refreshes and imperceptibly dissipates that which it wishes to maintain and elevate by its humidity. As the philosopher **Calid** <note>Calid: Referring to Khalid ibn Yazid (died c. 704), an Umayyad prince often cited as the first Arab alchemist. Many Latin treatises were attributed to him during the Middle Ages.</note> says: *the lesser fire wears down all things.* <note>original: *minor ignis omnia terit*. It is the way to hope for a laudable end from the very beginning of the labor undertaken: to provide it with a **tempered heat** which, without burning, penetrates so vividly into the bowels of this massive body that it softens its hardness and makes it bend to its every will—like water which, over time and through the continuity of its patience, carves out the firmest rocks, which it would never do by open force. The matter, altered and steadily heated, no longer retains its luster except in potential; and changing its beautiful complexion, it covers itself with an obscure, infinitely black veil, which makes it like a leper and <note>The "black veil" and "leper" are metaphors for the *Nigredo* or "Black Work." <summary>This page explains that successful alchemy requires a gentle, persistent heat rather than a violent flame, comparing its action to water wearing away stone. This process leads the matter to lose its metallic luster and enter the "black" stage of putrefaction.</summary>

**Calcination** <term>Calcination: The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in order to purify it or reduce it to a powder; in alchemy, this is often the first step in "killing" the raw metal to prepare it for rebirth.</term> or **dealbation** <term>Dealbation: From the Latin *dealbare* (to whiten). Natural axioms and principles assure us that universal corruption <note>In alchemy, "corruption" refers to the process of putrefaction, where matter is broken down to its base state so it can be reborn into something more perfect.</note> is the common sperm <note>original: "sperme"; used here to mean the fundamental "seed" or life-force inherent in all matter.</note>, the cement, and the proper seed for all generations. But if after so many subtle flights, you can stop it and correct its lightness by restraining the course of its speed <note>This describes the alchemical process of "fixation," where a volatile substance is made stable and solid so it can withstand the fire.</note>, you will be able to obtain, by just title of perpetual **emphyteusis** <note>A legal term for a very long-term lease of land; the author uses it metaphorically to mean a lasting, rightful claim to the riches of nature.</note>, very rich minerals, and enjoy for many years many precious things, whose exquisite value had not yet come to your perfect knowledge.

But it must also be known that the perfection of the entire <term>mastery: also known as the "Magistery," referring to the successful completion of the alchemical Great Work</term> depends upon this single point: that one must extract the <term>sulfur: in alchemy, the active, masculine principle representing the "soul" and the power of combustion or color</term> out of the perfect body having a fixed nature. For this sulfur is the most ancient and most subtle part of the <term>crystalline salt: a purified mineral state representing the "body" or "base" of the work</term>, of a sweet flavor, delectable to the taste, and of an aromatic humidity. These, having been within the fire for the space of a year, will always appear like melted wax; and therefore some part of it remains within the <term>quicksilver: mercury in its liquid, metallic form</term>, dyeing it into a most pure gold. For this reason, the humidity or water that one draws from the bodies of metals is called the soul of this <term>Stone: the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold</term>, hidden within the said humidity. For this water is called <term>spirit: the volatile, life-giving principle of a substance</term>, and the virtue of said spirit is called the soul and <term>tincture: the concentrated essence capable of "staining" or transforming the quality of a metal</term>, which dyes and <term>fixes: the process of making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates when heated</term> all of the said water into pure gold. But the Mercury, or the force and vigor thereof, is also called the spirit.

# ->The Golden Fleece<-
400

*By an acquiescence both free and voluntary,*
*This power is in every way beyond the ordinary.*

It is true that these virtues are such that the majority, being unable to grasp them fully, deny them this belief, regarding them as something impossible and beyond natural conception. Thus, the coarse ignorance of these flighty minds—unwilling to acknowledge in another what exceeds their own common opinion—imagines it can restrain the superhuman minutiae <note>original: "minutes"; refers to the small, precise details or the momentary perfections of the alchemical process.</note> of these perfections, and "rivet the nail" <note>original: "riuer le cloud"; a French idiom meaning to settle an argument or silence a critic. Here, the skeptics try to use their disbelief to invalidate alchemical truths.</note> of such a grand privilege through the decrees of some incredulous soul,

> *Beneath the weak compass of a vain appearance,*
> *If the result of a success <note>original: "bonheur"; implies a "good hap" or the fortunate completion of the alchemical Work.</note>, and if experience*
> *Did not point out this presumption to them with a finger.*

Or [if they] did not raise the nose of overweening pride <term>Overweening pride: "Outrecuidance" in the original French; a term for excessive arrogance or presumption, particularly regarding the limits of what is possible in nature.</term> at those bizarre souls, poisoned by a fleeting scrupulosity and by an error more than panic-stricken and profane,

**Vocabulary:**
The Golden Fleece, acquiescence, power, belief, conception, ignorance, opinion, perfection, privilege, overweening pride, bizarre, scruple.

<summary>The author critiques skeptics who dismiss alchemical truths as impossible because they transcend common natural understanding. He argues that only the evidence of successful experience can overcome the "overweening pride" and ignorance of those who doubt the superhuman perfections of the Art.</summary>

...have also treated [the subject] only through enigmatic figures, in obscure words, hyperbolic conversations and dialogues, or veiled similitudes, so that such a beautiful pearl <note>A continuation of the metaphor from the previous page, referencing the "pearl of great price" and the biblical warning not to cast it before those who cannot appreciate it.</note> could not be contaminated by the impure sacrifices <note>original: "holocaustes" — while today meaning a mass slaughter, here it refers to the ancient sense of "burnt offerings" or the clumsy, "heavy-handed" attempts of the uninitiated to perform alchemical work.</note> of base and unsanctified persons, as this most sacred mystery requires. Weak-hearted souls do not dare to undertake the long pursuit of the steps of Virtue, because it seems to them to be of difficult access and a painful conquest; whereas spirits of noble birth <note>original: "genereusement nais" — implying a natural nobility of character and intellect rather than just social rank.</note>, who do not degenerate from the legitimate eagle <term>legitimate eagle: a reference to the ancient belief that only true, noble eagles could look directly into the sun without blinking. # The Golden Fleece.

...ed at the summit of the Mountain of their felicities, to triumph happily over the fertile harvest and the labors sown in the soil of their perseverance, which finally attains the glorious palms of victory. The valor of the Argonauts <note>The mythological heroes who sailed with Jason to retrieve the Golden Fleece, serving here as an allegory for alchemists seeking the Stone.</note> could not be diverted from their celebrated enterprise by the perilous Syrtes <note>original: "Syrthes" — dangerous quicksands off the North African coast; used here metaphorically for the hidden traps and failures in alchemical practice.</note> that sought to deprive them of the joy of their conquest; instead, they pursued it with the sharpest constancy, through which their virtue became immortal. Thus they were not disappointed in the sweet fruit of their hoped-for glory, since Time, which brings all things to pass <note>original: "le tẽps ameine-tout" — a personification of Time as the ultimate revealer and provider of results.</note>, at last placed in their hands that precious jewel which a stay-at-home soul <note>original: "vne ame cafaniere" — literally a "house-bound soul," referring to those who lack the courage or spirit for difficult spiritual or intellectual journeys.</note> would never have dared to promise itself; nor would such a soul have set its sail to the wind amidst the uncertainty of the raging waves to seek the honorable prize <note>original: "defpoüille" — meaning "spoils" or "hide," a direct reference to the golden fleece of the ram.</note> of such rich spoils. We may judge the same of our work; the choice...

<summary>Using the myth of Jason and the Argonauts, the author argues that the "precious jewel" of alchemy is only attainable through unwavering constancy and the courage to face dangerous metaphorical "seas." It contrasts the successful philosopher with the timid "stay-at-home" soul who fears the risks of the Great Work.</summary>

<keywords>Argonauts, Syrtes, Golden Fleece, perseverance, constancy, Time, alchemical allegory, courage</keywords>

# The Golden Fleece.

...made of the Mariners <note>original: "Nautonniers" — sailors or navigators, here referring to the Alchemists who navigate the "sea" of the Great Work.</note> chosen for this task in the Council of the Heavens; yet they do not reach it, nor carry it away, except after a long labor supported by patience to soften the heart of our Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, personified here as a guarded entity that must be "softened" or prepared through the alchemist's devotion.</note>. This Stone well knows how to distinguish, from within the common and confused Economy <term>Economy: The organized system, management, or natural order of the universe.</term> of this wide universe, those whom she wishes to retain in her service, giving herself to them only after having first and maturely examined their consciences. She prudently extracts the secrets <note>original: "tiré les vers du nez" — literally "pulled the worms from the nose," a French idiom for skillfully extracting the truth or secrets from a person.</note> of their discretion, in order to make of it a ferment <note>In alchemy, a "ferment" is a substance (like yeast in bread) added to the "base" material to catalyze its transformation into gold or the Elixir.</note> favorable to her greatness. For she takes her time to allow herself to be conquered by the faithful perseverance of these wise Knights of the Fleece, to whom alone she communicates herself—not indifferently to everyone, nor even at all times, but rather in a certain season. For she awaits her time: when the golden stalks of grain reach maturity, and when the fruit of the earth has already

<summary>The author explains that the Philosopher's Stone is a selective entity that only reveals itself to "Mariners" or "Knights" who have been divinely chosen and proven through patience and moral integrity. The process is compared to a harvest that requires a specific "season" and "ferment" to reach maturity.</summary>

# The Golden Fleece.

...been preserved for several years, and until the settled minds of its co-heirs are capable of receiving this nuptial dowry <note>original: "dot nuptial" — a metaphor for the union of the alchemical substances (the "King" and "Queen") and the resulting reward given to the practitioner.</note>.

*For Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath often considered the father of early chemistry and alchemy.</note> says that they were old,*
*The Philosophers who possessed it:*
*And yet in their final days,*
*They enjoyed their loves.*

At which age especially, prudence and true <term>integrity</term> <note>original: "preud’hommie" — a Middle French term denoting uprightness, moral excellence, and the wisdom that comes with experience.</note>, or never at all, become familiar to men; those who, in this graying time of life, must have declared bankruptcy on the garments of a too-hasty youth. And that is why <term>Senior</term> <note>Senior Zadith (Zadith ben Hamuel) was a 10th-century Arab alchemist. His texts were foundational to the medieval European understanding of the "Great Work."</note> says that the man of wit and good judgment can easily understand the true means of happily reaching the <term>Cape of Good Hope</term> <note>original: "Cap d’eſperance" — here used as a metaphor for the successful conclusion of the alchemical process, comparing the alchemist's journey to the treacherous but rewarding maritime voyages of the era.</note> of this art, when he gives himself entirely and without cease to the reading of the good Authors, by means of which he shall be illuminated, and shall find...

<summary>The author argues that alchemical success is reserved for those of mature age and moral integrity, citing authorities like Geber and Senior Zadith. He compares the alchemist's journey to a sea voyage toward a "Cape of Good Hope," achievable only through dedicated study of classic texts.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Senior Zadith, maturity, integrity, Cape of Good Hope, nuptial dowry, Great Work, alchemy</keywords>

# The Golden Fleece.

...easy entry to finally arrive at the true knowledge of this divine Secret <note>original: "Secret" — in alchemy, the "Secret" or "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) refers to the mysterious process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>: thus a certain modern author holds in this following quatrain <note>A stanza consisting of four lines.</note>, in conformity with all the good essays of the true science <note>original: "vraye science" — here referring to alchemy, which practitioners considered the highest form of natural philosophy.</note>.

*Often gray hair releases the Birds,* <note>In alchemical symbolism, "birds" often represent volatile substances or spirits rising during the process of sublimation or distillation.</note>
*Which the Saturnian lodges in our Vessels:* <note>The "Saturnian" refers to Saturn, the planetary and alchemical association for lead, often representing the heavy, base material at the start of the work. The "vessels" are the specialized glassware used by the alchemist.</note>
*And the vivacity of the fleeting Mercury,*
*Is never tamed except in the mind of the wise.*

->THE END.<-

<summary>The text concludes by emphasizing that the "divine secret" of alchemy is mastered through the wisdom of age and experience, using a quatrain to illustrate the taming of volatile substances like Mercury.</summary>

<image-desc>A full-page marbled paper pattern, specifically a "stone" or "agate" marble design common in historical bookbindings. The pattern consists of large, organic, and irregular rounded "cells" in shades of light tan and beige. These shapes are delineated by thick, branching, and interconnected veins of dark chocolate brown and black, creating a cracked earth or biological cell-like appearance. Within the larger beige areas, there are subtle tonal variations and smaller, paler spots.</image-desc>

<term>Marbled paper</term> <note>A decorative technique where pigments are floated on a liquid bath and manipulated into patterns before being transferred to paper. This was a standard practice for high-quality bookbindings in the early modern period.</note>
<term>Endpaper</term> <note>The sheet of paper at the very beginning or end of a book, used to hide the raw edges of the cover material and provide a finished look to the interior.</note>
<term>Stone marble pattern</term> <note>A specific style of marbling intended to mimic the appearance of natural minerals like agate or polished stone.</note>

<summary>This final page consists of a decorative stone-patterned marbled endpaper, marking the physical conclusion of the volume.</summary>
